Approaching the Continuous from the Discrete: an Infinite Tensor Product Construction
Antonio Lorenzin, Fabio Zanasi

Abstract
Increasingly in recent years probabilistic computation has been investigated through the lenses of categorical algebra, especially via string diagrammatic calculi. Whereas categories of discrete and Gaussian probabilistic processes have been thoroughly studied, with various axiomatisation results, more expressive classes of continuous probability are less understood, because of the intrinsic difficulty of describing infinite behaviour by algebraic means. In this work, we establish a universal construction that adjoins infinite tensor products, allowing continuous probability to be investigated from discrete settings. Our main result applies this construction to 𝖥𝗂𝗇𝖲𝗍𝗈𝖼𝗁 \mathsf{FinStoch} , the category of finite sets and stochastic matrices, obtaining a category of locally constant Markov kernels, where the objects are finite sets plus the Cantor space 2 ℕ 2^{\mathbb{N}} . Any probability measure on the reals can be reasoned about in this category. Furthermore, we show how to lift axiomatisation results through the infinite tensor product construction. This way we obtain an axiomatic presentation of continuous probability over countable powers of 2 = { 0 , 1 } 2=\{0,1\} .



1
Introduction

Category-theoretic approaches to probabilistic computation have attracted growing interest in recent years, with applications ranging from evidential decision theory (
[
8
]
) to random graphs (
[
1
]
) and active inference (
[
28
]
).
Their ability to highlight the underlying algebraic structures provides a rigorous semantics that enhances formal clarity, compositional methodologies, and admits an intuitive description in terms of
string diagrams
(
[
22
,
24
]
). In particular, fundamental notions such as determinism, Bayesian inversion, and Bayesian updates can be studied algebraically in the diagrammatic language (
[
11
,
19
,
6
]
).

Following this emerging line of research, recent developments have focused on providing
complete axiomatisations
of categorical models of probability, via string diagrammatic theories. Completeness is key to guarantee that any semantic equality, and thus algebraic reasoning about the aforementioned notions, can in principle be derived in the diagrammatic calculus. In this context, axiomatising a (symmetric monoidal) category
{\mathsf{C}}
, which expresses our semantic domain, means to identify a set of generating string diagrams
\Sigma
and equations
E
such that the category
\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E)
freely obtained by the theory
(\Sigma,E)
is isomorphic (or just equivalent) to
{\mathsf{C}}
. In other words,
{\mathsf{C}}
-morphisms may be regarded as
\Sigma
-diagrams quotiented by
E
. A first such result is the axiomatisation of the category of finite sets and stochastic matrices (with monoidal product given by disjoint sum) via the theory of convex algebras, presented in
[
10
]
following
[
26
]
. Another such result is contained in
[
25
]
, which studies a category of Gaussian probability using string diagrams expressing affine, relational, and quadratic behaviour. Most relevant to the present scope is the complete axiomatisation of a category of discrete probabilistic processes given in
[
21
]
. Specifically, the category under investigation, denoted by
\mathsf{BinStoch}
, is the category of stochastic matrices between powers of
2=\{0,1\}
, and the associated syntax is generated by probability distributions and Boolean operations.
Two related efforts generalise this description beyond
\mathsf{BinStoch}
:
[
23
]
extends the framework by introducing a graded structure to encompass imprecise probabilities, while
[
4
]
notes that the use of an additional monoidal product enables an axiomatisation of
\mathsf{FinStoch}
, the category of stochastic matrices between arbitrary finite sets.

To date, a fundamental gap in this research area is the absence of a string diagrammatic axiomatisation for categories of
continuous
probability beyond the Gaussian case. A chief example is
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
, whose objects are standard Borel spaces and whose morphisms are Markov kernels (generalisations of stochastic matrices to the continuous setting).
It is generally unclear how to encode infinite behaviour directly via a choice of generators and equations within the existing framework of string diagrammatic algebra.

In this work we address such challenge through the use of
infinite tensor products
, which are special limits of tensor (monoidal) products.
1
1

1

Throughout, we use “tensor” and “monoidal” synonymously, in order to both follow the terminology of
[
16
]
and adhere to the terminology of categorical probability.

This approach is inspired by
[
16
]
, where it is shown that
\mathbb{R}
is the infinite tensor product of finite sets in
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
.
In particular, the celebrated Kolmogorov extension theorem ensures that all probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
can be seen as limits of probability measures on finite sets. Our main contributions are the following:

1.

Introduce a universal construction that adjoins infinite tensor products (
Theorem

3.1
);

2.

Employ this construction to describe freely generated categories with an infinite tensor product, which admit a diagrammatic representation through plate notation (
Section

4
);

3.

Provide a characterisation of the category
{\mathsf{FinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
obtained by adjoining infinite tensor products to
\mathsf{FinStoch}
(
Theorem

6.1
) in terms of Stone spaces and locally constant Markov kernels.

4.

Provide an axiomatic presentation of
\mathsf{CantorStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
, the restriction of
{\mathsf{FinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
to powers of
2
together with the Cantor space
2^{\mathbb{N}}
, its (countably) infinite power (
Corollary

2
).

Regarding the third contribution, one might hope that the whole of
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
could be recovered as
{\mathsf{FinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
, i.e. by adjoining infinite tensor products to its discrete counterpart
\mathsf{FinStoch}
.
However, this is overly ambitious: not because of probability measures, which can always be described as above, but because of the freeness of measurable functions, whose behaviour cannot be fully captured by finite operations. What we obtain instead is a subcategory of
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
, which is nonetheless quite expressive: probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
are in bijective correspondence with probability measures on
2^{\mathbb{N}}
, thus showing that
\mathsf{CantorStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
is rich enough to encompass all probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
.
Additionally, this category includes nontrivial kernels that capture biased behaviour with respect to their inputs (see
Example

7
for a detailed discussion).

Outline

First we recall semicartesian categories, which provide the necessary structure to define infinite tensor products (
Section

2
).
In
Section

3
we give a universal construction of the category obtained by adjoining infinite tensor products to a semicartesian category.
String diagrams and monoidal theories are discussed in
Section

4
, while
Section

5
restricts the focus to Markov categories.

Section

6
presents the main result, giving an explicit description of the universal construction for
\mathsf{FinStoch}
and
\mathsf{BinStoch}
.
The developed syntax is applied in
Section

7
to a short use case on Markov chains.
Concluding remarks and future directions are briefly discussed in
Section

8
.
Proofs of auxiliary results are deferred to the appendices.

2
Semicartesian Categories and Infinite Tensor Products

For the sake of generality, we will first consider semicartesian categories (sometimes known as affine symmetric monoidal categories), as their theory is rich enough to allow the study of infinite tensor products (
[
16
]
).
From
Section

5
onward, we will restrict focus to Markov categories, broadly adopted for modelling probabilistic and statistical behaviour — see, e.g.,
[
11
,
7
,
20
,
18
]
.
In fact, the examples introduced below are all Markov categories.

Definition 1

A
semicartesian category
is a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{C},\otimes,I)
in which the monoidal unit
I
is terminal.
The unique morphism of type
X\to I
is denoted by
\del_{X}
(in string diagrams,

) and called
delete morphism
.

Example 1

The main example for our purposes is the semicartesian category
\mathsf{FinStoch}

[
11
, Example 2.5]
, whose objects are finite sets and whose morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
are functions
Y\times X\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
satisfying
\Sigma_{y\in Y}f(y,x)=1
, also known as stochastic functions.
To better highlight the connection with probability theory, one generally employs the “conditional notation” and write
f(y\,|\,x)
in place of
f(y,x)
.
In particular,
1=\Sigma_{y\in Y}f(y,x)=\Sigma_{y\in Y}f(y\,|\,x)
means that
f(-\,|\,x)
is a probability distribution on
Y
for each
x\in X
.
Composition is defined by
g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f(z\,|\,x):=\sum_{y\in Y}g(z\,|\,y)f(y\,|\,x)
for any composable morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
, .
while the tensor product is given by
f\otimes h(y,w\,|\,x,z):=f(y\,|\,x)h(w\,|\,z)
for any
f\colon X\to Y
and
h\colon Z\to W
.
In particular, the delete maps are given by
\del({\,|\,x})=1
.

Example 2

Another important example is
\mathsf{BinStoch}
, the full subcategory of
\mathsf{FinStoch}
given by finite powers of
2=\{0,1\}
.
This has become especially relevant after the work
[
21
]
provided a complete axiomatisation for such a category.

Example 3

In
[
16
]
, where infinite tensor products were originally defined, the main example is
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
, which generalizes
\mathsf{FinStoch}
outside the finite context.

\mathsf{BorelStoch}
is the category whose objects are standard Borel spaces (measurably isomorphic to a finite set,
\mathbb{Z}
, or
\mathbb{R}
by Kuratowski’s theorem) and whose morphisms are Markov kernels
f\colon X\to Y
, given by honest functions
f\colon\Sigma_{Y}\times X\to[0,1]
, where
\Sigma_{Y}
is the
\sigma
-algebra of
Y
, such that
f(U\,|\,-)\colon X\to[0,1]
is measurable and
f(-\,|\,x)\colon\Sigma_{Y}\to[0,1]
is a probability measure.

The measurability condition allows us to define a composition by integration:
g\mathchoice{\,}{\,}{}{}f(U\,|\,x):=\int_{Y}g(U\,|\,dy)f(dy\,|\,x)
.
The tensor product is determined by the rule
f\otimes h(U\times V\,|\,x,z)\coloneqq f(U\,|\,x)h(V\,|\,z)
.
As before, the delete maps are given by
\del_{X}(-\,|\,x)=1
for all
x\in X
.

Example 4

A more general version of
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
is
\mathsf{Stoch}
, where objects are all measurable spaces. See
[
11
]
for details.

Infinite tensor products were introduced in
[
16
]
to formulate zero–one laws in categorical language.
They have since become central in category-theoretic approaches to continuous probability; for instance,
[
13
]
employs them in discussing laws of large numbers. We now recall the basic setup and introduce some new terminology that will be convenient for later use. Throughout, let
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J)
denote the category whose objects are finite subsets of a
countable
set
J
, with a morphism
F\to F^{\prime}
whenever
F\subseteq F^{\prime}
.

Definition 2

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a semicartesian category. A functor
X\colon\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
is an
abstract infinite tensor product
2
2

2

To be precise, we should say abstract
countable
infinite tensor product. To avoid overloading notation, we will simply omit the adjective, and throughout infinite will always mean
countable
infinite. Nonetheless, the full generality is expected to work, and this restriction is important only to our main application.
if there exists a family of objects
(X_{j})_{j\in J}
such that
X
sends
F
to
X_{F}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{j\in F}X_{j}
and the inclusion
F\subseteq F^{\prime}
to the morphism

\pi_{F^{\prime},F}\colon X_{F^{\prime}}\to X_{F}

which deletes all
j\in F^{\prime}\setminus F
, and keeps the rest unchanged. Up to permutation,
\pi_{F^{\prime},F}=\mathrm{id}_{X_{F}}\otimes\del_{X_{F^{\prime}\setminus F}}
.

Intuitively, an abstract infinite tensor product is a choice of an infinite number of objects in
\mathsf{C}
.

Notation 1

Whenever
X
is an abstract infinite tensor product, its associated countable set
J
may be denoted by
J_{X}
for clarity.

Definition 3

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a semicartesian category.
A
(concrete) infinite tensor product

X=\bigotimes_{j\in J}X_{j}
for a family of objects
(X_{j})_{j\in J}
in
\mathsf{C}
is a limit of the associated abstract infinite tensor product, provided this limit is preserved by
-\otimes Y
for all
Y\in\mathsf{C}
.

The morphisms
X\to X_{F}
given by the universal property will be called
finite marginalizations
and denoted by
\pi_{F}
.

Note that such products are not strictly unique; rather, they are unique up to isomorphism. This subtle distinction mirrors the one between strict and strong monoidal functors, and it is the reason we will work with the latter throughout.

Definition 4

A semicartesian category
has infinite tensor products
if every abstract infinite tensor is realised, i.e. it is associated to a concrete infinite tensor product.

In
[
16
]
, it was shown that
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
has infinite tensor products, and moreover
\mathbb{R}
is isomorphic to any nontrivial infinite tensor product (this follows by the mentioned Kuratowski’s theorem, since
\mathbb{R}
is measurably isomorphic to any uncountable standard Borel space).

Throughout, symmetric monoidal functors are always meant to be
strong
, meaning that their coherence morphisms are always isomorphisms.

Definition 5

A symmetric monoidal functor
\phi\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
between two semicartesian categories with infinite tensor products is
ITP-preserving
if
\phi(\bigotimes_{j\in J}X_{j})
is an infinite tensor product of the family
(\phi(X_{j}))_{j\in J}
.
3
3

3

In particular, the finite marginalizations
\pi_{F}
are preserved by
\phi
.
As
\pi_{F}
is a notation for any infinite tensor product, we will write
\phi(\pi_{F})=\pi_{F}
for simplicity.

Before proceeding, we also consider the following technical requirement, necessary to establish the forthcoming results. In
[
17
]
, semicartesian categories are called theories and the property below is called normality.
Following a now-established tradition in categorical modelling of probability
[
6
,
11
,
22
]
, we will often depict morphisms of these categories using string diagrams.

Definition 6

A semicartesian category
\mathsf{C}
has
cancellative deletions
if
f\otimes\del_{X}=g\otimes\del_{X}
implies
f=g
for all parallel morphisms
f,g
and all objects
X
.
In string diagrams,

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.8}{0.0}{0.0}{0.8}{14.22615pt}{5.69046pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.8}{0.0}{0.0}{0.8}{11.38092pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

for all
f
and
g
.

In particular, surjectiveness of the marginalizations
\pi_{F^{\prime},F}\colon X_{F^{\prime}}\to X_{F}
and
\pi_{F}\colon X\to X_{F}
above is ensured.

The fact that
\mathsf{FinStoch}
,
\mathsf{BinStoch}
,
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
and
\mathsf{Stoch}
satisfy this property follows by direct check:
whenever
f\otimes\del_{X}(U\,|\,a,x)=g\otimes\del_{X}(U\,|\,a,x)
, then
f(U\,|\,a)=f(U\,|\,a)\del_{X}(\,|\,x)=g(U\,|\,a)\del_{X}(\,|\,x)=g(U\,|\,a)
concludes the proof since
\del_{X}(\,|\,x)=1
for all
x
. Note that to ensure this property, the emptyset is not an object of these categories.

3
A Universal Construction for Infinite Tensor Products

We now focus on proving the following statement, which intuitively states that adjoining infinite tensor products satisfies a natural universal property.
This result is propaedeutic for our aims, but it is also of independent interest for other works using infinite tensor products
[
5
,
14
,
16
]
.

Theorem 3.1

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a semicartesian category with cancellative deletions.
Then there exist a semicartesian category
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
with infinite tensor products and a strict symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{C}\to{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
, such that for every semicartesian category
\mathsf{D}
with infinite tensor products and every symmetric monoidal functor
\phi\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
, there exists an ITP-preserving symmetric monoidal functor
\tilde{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}\to\mathsf{D}
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\phi}}

Moreover, such a
\tilde{\phi}
is unique up to natural isomorphism.

First, we define what
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
is. Its objects will be abstract infinite tensor products.
However, morphisms are a little bit more subtle, as they should allow these tensor products to be limits in
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
.
This leads to the following.

Definition 7

Let
X\colon\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{X})\to\mathsf{C}
and
Y\colon\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{Y})\to\mathsf{C}
be two abstract infinite tensor products.
A
compatible family

f\colon X\to Y
is given by an index set
\Lambda_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{X})\times\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{Y})
and a collection
(f_{F,G}\colon X(F)\to Y(G))_{(F,G)\in\Lambda_{f}}
subject to the following requests:

•

(Naturality)
For every
F^{\prime}\supseteq F
and
G^{\prime}\supseteq G
, if
(F,G),(F^{\prime},G^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{f}
, then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(F^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(G^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F^{\prime},F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{G^{\prime},G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{F,G}}

commutes;

•

(Covering condition)
For every
G\in\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{Y})
, there exists
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{X})
such that
(F,G)\in\Lambda_{f}
;

•

(Hereditariness)
If
(F,G)\in\Lambda_{f}
, then
(F^{\prime},G)\in\Lambda_{f}
for every
F^{\prime}\supseteq F
.

We moreover say that two compatible families
f,f^{\prime}\colon X\to Y
are
equivalent
if
f_{F,G}=f^{\prime}_{F,G}
for all
(F,G)
in the intersection of the index sets
\Lambda_{f}\cap\Lambda_{f^{\prime}}
.

Hereditariness above (together with cancellativity of deletions) ensures transitivity of the equivalence defined for compatible families; in particular, the covering condition also holds for
\Lambda_{f}\cap\Lambda_{f^{\prime}}
.

We are finally ready to define
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
.
To obtain a strict monoidal functor
\mathsf{C}\to{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
, we single out an element
*
, common to all countable sets
J
considered.

Definition 8

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a semicartesian category with cancellative deletion, and let
*
be a set.
Then
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
is the semicartesian category defined as follows:

•

Each object is an abstract infinite tensor product
X\colon\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
, with
*\in J
;

•

Morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
are equivalence classes of compatible families;

•

Composition is defined pointwise:
gf
is the compatible family given by

gf_{F,H}\coloneqq g_{G,H}f_{F,G}

whenever there is a finite subset
G
;

•

The monoidal product of
X
and
Y
is given by

\begin{array}[]{cccc}X\otimes Y\colon&\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{X}\cup J_{Y})&\longrightarrow&\mathsf{C}\\
&F&\longmapsto&X(F\cap J_{X})\otimes Y(F\cap J_{Y}),\end{array}

and consequently, given
f\colon X\to Z
and
g\colon Y\to W
,
f\otimes g
is the morphism such that

(f\otimes g)_{F,G}\coloneqq f_{F\cap J_{X},G\cap J_{Z}}\otimes g_{F\cap J_{Y},G\cap J_{W}}.

(The monoidal unit is given by
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(\{*\})\owns\{*\}\mapsto I\in\mathsf{C}
).

The composition is thoroughly studied in
Appendix

0.B
, where it is shown that
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
is a semicartesian category.
In particular, the request of having cancellative deletions ensures that the composition is well-defined.

By construction, there exists a fully faithful symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{C}\to{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
, given by considering each object
X
of
\mathsf{C}
as a functor
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(\{*\})\to\mathsf{C}
defined by
\{*\}\mapsto X
.
We note that
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
is strict if
\mathsf{C}
is, since the monoidal product is described pointwise.

Proposition 1

{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
has infinite tensor products.

Proof

We start by claiming that
X\colon\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{X})\to\mathsf{C}
is the infinite tensor product of the family
X(G)
, with
G\in\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{X})
, in
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
.
Indeed, consider a morphism
f\colon A\to X
.
By definition, this yields a family of morphisms
f_{G}\colon A\to X_{G}
indexed by
G
.
Conversely, if one has a family of morphisms
f_{G}\colon A\to X_{G}
such that
\pi_{G^{\prime},G}f_{G^{\prime}}=f_{G}
for all
G^{\prime}\supseteq G
, then one can easily construct a compatible family
f_{F,G}\colon A(F)\to X(G)
, uniquely defined up to equivalence. The fact that the infinite tensor product is preserved under tensoring follows by the generality of the argument.

Now, given any countable family
(X_{k})_{k\in K}
in
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
, consider the set
H\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{k\in K}J_{X_{k}}
, which is countable because it is a countable union of countable sets.
We claim that the abstract infinite tensor product
Y\colon\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(H)\to\mathsf{C}
given by
F\mapsto\bigotimes_{k\in K}X_{k}(F\cap J_{X_{k}})
is indeed the infinite tensor product of
(X_{k})_{k\in K}
.
Starting with
f\colon A\to Y
, by definition of morphisms in
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
, one obtains
f_{F}\colon A\to\bigotimes_{k\in F}X_{k}
for all
F\in\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(K)
.
Conversely, given a family
(f_{F}\colon A\to\bigotimes_{k\in F}X_{k})_{F\in\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(K)}
such that
\pi_{F^{\prime},F}f_{F^{\prime}}=f_{F}
, the fact that every
X_{k}
is an infinite tensor product ensures that each
f_{F}
is determined by
(f_{F}^{G}\colon A\to\bigotimes_{k\in F}X_{G\cap J_{X_{k}}})
, with
G
a finite subset of
\bigsqcup_{k\in F}J_{X_{k}}
.
In particular,
f
determines a family of morphisms indexed by all finite subsets of
H=\bigsqcup_{k\in K}J_{X_{k}}
, giving rise to a compatible family
A\to Y
.
As wanted,
Y
is the infinite tensor product of
(X_{k})_{k\in K}
.

Finally, we prove of the main statement of the section.

Proof
(Proof of
Theorem

3.1
)

Given
\phi\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
a symmetric monoidal functor, then an abstract infinite tensor product
X\colon\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J)\to\mathsf{C}
immediately gives rise to an abstract infinite tensor product on
\mathsf{D}
by composition and coherence isomorphisms. Since
\mathsf{D}
has infinite tensor products, this is realized as a concrete infinite tensor product, denoted by
\tilde{\phi}(X)
and equipped with finite marginalizations
\pi_{F}\colon\tilde{\phi}(X)\to\phi(X)_{F}
.

Now, given a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
, by definition this is a compatible family
(f_{F,G}\colon X(F)\to Y(G))
. By the properties,
\phi(f_{F,G})
gives rise to a morphism
\tilde{\phi}(f)_{G}\colon\tilde{\phi}(X)\to\phi(Y)_{G}
given by the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\phi}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(X)_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(Y)_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(f_{F,G})}

(1)

and naturality ensures that
\tilde{\phi}(f)_{G}
is a family that factors through the limit of
\phi(Y)_{G}
, which by definition is
\tilde{\phi}(Y)
; in other words, we obtain a morphism
\tilde{\phi}(X)\to\tilde{\phi}(Y)
, which we denote by
\tilde{\phi}(f)
.

Proving that
\tilde{\phi}
so defined is a symmetric monoidal functor is now a simple routine check, and it is ITP-preserving by construction.
Moreover, its uniqueness up to natural isomorphism follows from the universal property of limits and the description of morphisms provided by (
1
) (note it is not only sufficient to describe a choice of morphisms, but necessary).

Proposition 2

Let
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
be two semicartesian categories with cancellative deletions, and assume
\mathsf{D}
has infinite tensor products.
If a symmetric monoidal functor
\phi\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
is faithful, then so is
\tilde{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}\to\mathsf{D}
.

Proof

Let
f,g\colon X\to Y
be two parallel morphisms in
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
such that
\tilde{\phi}(f)=\tilde{\phi}(g)
. Consider their marginalizations
\pi_{G}\tilde{\phi}(f)=\pi_{F}\tilde{\phi}(g)\colon\tilde{\phi}(X)\to\tilde{\phi}(Y)_{G}
.
For any
G
, there exists
F\subseteq J_{X}
such that
\pi_{G}f=f_{F,G}\pi_{F}
because
f
is a compatible family, and analogously for
g
. Moreover,
F
can be chosen for both
f
and
g
by covering condition and hereditariness, so also
\pi_{G}g=g_{F,G}\pi_{F}
.
Using this equality via
\tilde{\phi}
, we obtain

\phi(f_{F,G})\pi_{F}=\tilde{\phi}(f_{F,G}\pi_{F})=\tilde{\phi}(\pi_{G}f)=\pi_{G}\tilde{\phi}(f)=\\
\pi_{G}\tilde{\phi}(g)=\tilde{\phi}(\pi_{G}g)=\tilde{\phi}(g_{F,G}\pi_{F})=\phi(g_{F,G})\pi_{F}

Since
\mathsf{D}
has cancellative deletions,
\pi_{F}
is an epimorphism (note that
\pi_{F}=\mathrm{id}_{X_{F}}\otimes\del_{X_{J_{X}\setminus F}}
up to permutations), and therefore
\phi(f_{F,G})=\phi(g_{F,G})
.
By faithfulness of
\phi
, we conclude that
f_{F,G}=g_{F,G}
.
Arbitrariness of
G
, together with naturality of compatible families, implies that
f
and
g
are equivalent compatible families, i.e.
f=g
.

4
String Diagrams and Monoidal Theories for Infinite Tensor Products

In this section we study how to derive presentations by generators and equations for
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
from those of
\mathsf{C}
. In doing so, we also introduce a string diagrammatic representation for morphisms of
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
which highlights their formulation as compatible families of
\mathsf{C}
-morphisms (
Definitions

7
and
8
). Our approach is reminiscent of the ‘plate’ notation commonly used in the study of Bayesian networks, see e.g.
[
3
]
.
In category-theoretic approaches to probability, this has recently been employed in
[
5
]
.
Our use of plate notation is similar in spirit, but it is tailored to ensure a formal representation of compatible families.

We recall that a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
is given by a compatible family
(f_{F,G}\colon X(F)\to Y(G))_{(F,G)\in\Lambda_{f}}
, with
f_{F,G}
in
\mathsf{C}
, which we write as

f_{F,G}
(F,G)\in\Lambda_{f}
X
Y

in plate notation.
This morphism may also be denoted by

f
X
Y

for brevity, where the double wire of the input and output of
f
indicate that
X
and
Y
are infinite tensor products.
For simplicity, we will generally omit
(F,G)\in\Lambda_{f}
, since the index set is determined by the compatible family.
This notation is compatible in the obvious way with sequential and parallel composition in
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
(see
Fig.

1
). Moreover, it will be convenient for graphical reasoning to allow plates to disappear when infinite tensors are not involved, i.e.
(f_{F,G}\colon X(F)\to Y(G))_{(F,G)\in\Lambda_{f}}
is a bona fide morphism of
\mathsf{C}
. More precisely,

Figure 1:
Sequential and parallel composition of the plate notation.

We can use plate notation to define a variation of the construction of a symmetric monoidal category from generators and equations, which encompasses infinite tensor products. This will be useful in the following sections to derive axiomatisation results.

First, recall that a
symmetric monoidal theory
(SMT) is a pair
(\Sigma,E)
, where
\Sigma
is a signature of generators
o\colon m\to n
with an arity
m\in\mathbb{N}
and coarity
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
E
is a set of equations between
\Sigma
-terms. A
\Sigma
-term
c
of type
m\to n
will be represented graphically as a box with
m
dangling wires on the left and
n
on the right, also written
c
m
n
. Formally,
\Sigma
-terms are freely obtained by sequential and parallel compositions of the generators in
\Sigma
together with the identity

and set of equations as in
[
21
, Figure 4]
is named
{\mathsf{CausCirc}}
in
[
21
]
, as we may regard its string diagrams as causal circuits. Also in
[
21
]
it is proven that
{\mathsf{CausCirc}}\cong\mathsf{BinStoch}
, meaning that such an SMT
axiomatises

\mathsf{BinStoch}
.

We note that
\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E)
is semicartesian with cancellative deletions if and only if both of the following are satisfied:

1.

There is only one
\Sigma
-term (up to
=_{E}
) of type
n\to 0
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

2.

Given two
\Sigma
-terms
f,g
,
f\otimes\del_{n}=_{E}g\otimes\del_{n}
if and only if
f=_{E}g
.

Under these assumptions, we may define how to freely obtain a category with infinite tensor products from
(\Sigma,E)
as follows.

Definition 9

Given an SMT
(\Sigma,E)
satisfying the two assumptions above, let
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)
be defined as the category (with infinite tensor products) whose objects are natural numbers together with an additional object
\infty
, and whose morphisms are given by compatible families of
\Sigma
-terms, subject to the equations of
Fig.

1
and (
2
).

The proof that
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)
is indeed a category follows by the same argument used to prove that
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
is a category (see
Appendix

0.B
).

Regarding the tensor product, one sets
\infty\otimes N=\infty
for any
N
natural number or
\infty
, and the tensor product is obtained by reindexing the compatible families according to chosen fixed bijections
\mathbb{N}\sqcup\{0,1,\dots,n-1\}\cong\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{N}\sqcup\mathbb{N}\cong\mathbb{N}
. These choices are necessary to ensure that associators, unitors and permutations are well-defined: for example, if we call
\phi
the bijection
\mathbb{N}\sqcup\mathbb{N}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{N}
, then the associator is a reindexing with respect to the bijection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}\sqcup\mathbb{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}\sqcup\mathbb{N}\sqcup\mathbb{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}\sqcup\mathbb{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\sqcup\phi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\sqcup\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Moreover, we can verify that
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)
indeed has infinite tensor products, by an argument analogous to the one showing
Proposition

1
.

We now state that the infinite tensor product construction of Definition
8
, when applied to a freely generated SMC
\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E)
, is presented by the same
(\Sigma,E)
via the
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(-)
construction above.

Proposition 3

Let
(\Sigma,E)
be a symmetric monoidal theory such that
\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E)
is a semicartesian category with cancellative deletions.
Then
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)\simeq{(\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E))}^{\otimes\infty}
via an ITP-preserving symmetric monoidal functor.

Proof

By applying
Theorem

3.1
and
Proposition

2
, we have a faithful functor
{\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E)}^{\otimes\infty}\to\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)
.
Fullness is immediate by definition of
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)
.

We emphasise that
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)
and
{(\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E))}^{\otimes\infty}
are
not
isomorphic.
In fact, these two categories reflect different trade-offs.
The former,
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)
, admits a single infinite tensor product
\infty
, which may appear more natural at first glance, but then
\infty\otimes\infty=\infty
requires to fix a chosen bijection
\mathbb{N}\sqcup\mathbb{N}\cong\mathbb{N}
, as mentioned above.
The latter,
{(\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E))}^{\otimes\infty}
, instead allows multiple infinite tensor products, with the advantage that its tensor product does not rely on any choice of bijections.
Regardless of this distinction, in both categories one can reason equationally using the SMT
(\Sigma,E)
together with the equations of
Fig.

1
and (
2
).

Combining
Theorems

3.1
and
3
, one can prove the expected universal property for the free construction.

Corollary 1

Let
(\Sigma,E)
be an SMT satisfying the two assumptions preceding
Definition

9
, and consider a semicartesian category
\mathsf{D}
with infinite tensor products.
Then a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E)\to\mathsf{D}
extends to an ITP-preserving symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)\to\mathsf{D}
.

In other words,
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)
is initial among the semicartesian categories with infinite tensor products in which
(\Sigma,E)
can be interpreted.

Proof

By
Theorem

3.1
,
\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E)\to\mathsf{D}
extends to
{(\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E))}^{\otimes\infty}\to\mathsf{D}
. By composing this functor with the equivalence
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)\simeq{(\mathsf{Free}(\Sigma,E))}^{\otimes\infty}
provided in
Proposition

3
, the statement follows.

5
Infinite Tensor Products and Markov Categories

As mentioned, our motivating examples are not just semicartesian but also Markov categories. Given the relevance of these structures in the categorical probability literature, it is of interest to show that whenever
\mathsf{C}
is a Markov category, then so is
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
.

Definition 10

A
Markov category
is a semicartesian category
\mathsf{C}
where every object
X
comes equipped with a cocommutative comonoid
\cop_{X}\colon X\to X\otimes X
(whose counit is given by
\del_{X}
) compatible with the monoidal product. In string diagrams,
\cop_{X}
is usually denoted by

and the cocommutative comonoid equations read as follows.

=

=

=
=

(The delete maps are obviously compatible since they are the determined by terminality of
I
).

As mentioned,
\mathsf{FinStoch}
,
\mathsf{BinStoch}
,
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
and
\mathsf{Stoch}
are Markov categories.
In
\mathsf{FinStoch}
,
\cop_{X}(y,z\,|\,x)\coloneqq 1
if
x=y=z
and
0
otherwise. More details can be found in
[
6
,
11
]
.

Proposition 4

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a Markov category with cancellative deletions. Then
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
is a Markov category as well.

Proof

For every abstract infinite tensor product
X
, the collection of copy maps
(\cop_{X(F)}\colon X(F)\to X(F)\otimes X(F))_{F\in\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{X})}
gives rise to a compatible family, thanks to compatibility with the monoidal product.
We then define
\cop_{X}
to be (the equivalence class of) such compatible family.
Graphically,

By
Fig.

1
, the cocommutative comonoid equations hold because they hold inside the plate. Similarly, compatibility with the monoidal product holds, therefore concluding the proof.

In particular, by construction of the copy of
X
, a direct computation shows that
X
is a Kolmogorov product in the sense of
[
16
]
.

6
A Stone Space Characterisation of
{\mathsf{FinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}

In this section we explicitly describe what
{\mathsf{FinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
and
{\mathsf{BinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
are, therefore highlighting what information about the continuous setting can be recovered from
\mathsf{FinStoch}
and
\mathsf{BinStoch}
. By
Proposition

2
, these infinite tensor products extensions can be viewed as subcategories of
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
.
With this in mind, it suffices to understand what is the relevant notion of Markov kernels associated to
{\mathsf{FinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
and
{\mathsf{BinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
.

To this end, we seek to find a category where everything is seemingly “finitely-determined”, in a way that resembles the notion of compatible families.
For this reason, we focus on
Stone spaces
.
We recall that these spaces are compact Hausdorff spaces with a base consisting of clopen sets.
In particular, we will denote by
\mathsf{Clopen}(X)
the clopen sets of a Stone space
X
.
These spaces are particularly well-known in the theory due to Stone duality, which states that
\mathsf{Clopen}(-)
yields a contravariant equivalence between the category of Stone spaces and continuous maps and the category of Boolean algebras and algebra homomorphisms.

In this setting, the most important Stone space for our purposes is the
Cantor space

2^{\mathbb{N}}
, which, as the notation suggests, is given by the product of countably many copies of
2=\{0,1\}
.
By Brouwer’s theorem, the Cantor space is the only non-empty Stone space that is second-countable and without isolated points. We now equip Stone spaces with a special type of Markov kernels.

Definition 11

Let
X
and
Y
be two Stone spaces. A
locally constant Markov kernel

X
to
Y
, denoted by
f\colon X\hbox to38pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.99997pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}Y
, is given by a function

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}f&\colon&\mathsf{Clopen}(Y)\times X&\to&[0,1]\\
&&(U,x)&\mapsto&f(U\,|\,x)\end{array}

such that

•

For all
U\in\mathsf{Clopen}(Y)
, the function
f(U\,|\,-)\colon X\to[0,1]
is locally constant, i.e. for every
x\in X
there exists an open
A\owns x
such that
f(U\,|\,x)=f(U\,|\,y)
for all
y\in A
;

•

For all
x\in X
, the function
f(-\,|\,x)\colon\mathsf{Clopen}(Y)\to[0,1]
is a finitely-additive probability measure, i.e.
f(Y\,|\,x)=1
and for every finite collection of disjoint clopen sets
\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,

f\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\,\bigg|\,x\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(U_{i}\,|\,x).

Stone spaces with locally constant Markov kernels form a Markov category denoted by
\mathsf{StoneStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
(see
Appendix

0.C
for details).

Remark 1

By Carathéodory’s extension theorem, a finitely-additive probability measure on clopen sets gives rise to a
\sigma
-additive probability measure on the generated
\sigma
-algebra, often called the Baire
\sigma
-algebra.
In particular, this allows us to integrate against the Markov kernels described above, as they are particular instances of the standard Markov kernels recalled in
Example

3
, where
f(-\,|\,x)
is required to be a probability measure on a
\sigma
-algebra.

Example 6

Not all Markov kernels are locally constant. For the sake of an example, we define a Markov kernel from the Cantor space
2^{\mathbb{N}}
to
2
given by

f(U\,|\,(x_{n}))\coloneqq\begin{cases}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2x_{n}}{3^{n+1}}&\text{if }U=\{1\}\\
1-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2x_{n}}{3^{n+1}}&\text{if }U=\{0\}\end{cases}

This is clearly not locally constant, since any choice of
(x_{n})
changes the value of
f(U\,|\,(x_{n}))
, although
(x_{n})\mapsto\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2x_{n}}{3^{n+1}}
is the standard continuous embedding
2^{\mathbb{N}}\hookrightarrow[0,1]
.

Nonetheless, all continuous functions yield locally constant Markov kernels by means of delta measures (see
Appendix

0.C
for details).
We now present two examples of locally constant Markov kernels that demonstrate the expressive power of this concept.

Example 7

Note that
\mathsf{Clopen}(2^{\mathbb{N}})
is the collections of sets of the form
U_{F}\times 2^{\mathbb{N}\setminus F}
, where
F\subseteq\mathbb{N}
is finite and
U_{F}\subseteq 2^{F}
is arbitrary.

, defined by

p(U_{F}\times 2^{\mathbb{N}\setminus F}\,|\,)\coloneqq\frac{\#(U_{F})}{2^{\#(F)}}

where

denotes the size of

.
This construction is similar to the Lebesgue measure on the real line, in that it is translation-invariant:
p(U_{F}\times 2^{\mathbb{N}\setminus F}\,|\,)=p(U_{G}\times 2^{\mathbb{N}\setminus G}\,|\,)
whenever
U_{F}
and
U_{G}
are in bijection.
The fact that it is locally constant is immediate, since
I
has only one element.

introduced in
Item

1
. We set

f(U\,|\,x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}p(U\,|\,)+\frac{1}{2}&\text{if }x\in U\\
\frac{1}{2}p(U\,|\,)&\text{if }x\notin U\end{cases}

Intuitively, the probability is biased toward the conditioning point
x
. The fact that
f(-\,|\,x)
is a finitely-additive probability measure follows from the same property of
p(-\,|\,)
. Moreover,
f(U\,|\,-)
is constant on
U
and on its complement, hence
f
is locally constant.

Proposition 5

\mathsf{StoneStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
, the category of Stone spaces and locally constant Markov kernels, has all infinite tensor products. More explicitly, the infinite tensor product of
(X_{j})_{j\in J}
is given by
\prod_{j\in J}X_{j}
.

Proof

First of all, we note that
X\coloneqq\prod_{j\in J}X_{j}
is a Stone space, with compactness ensured by Tychonoff’s theorem.
Now, let us consider
A
another Stone space, and consider
f\colon A\hbox to38pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.99997pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-22.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

a locally constant Markov kernel.
We claim that
f
is determined by the marginals
f_{F}
, given by the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{F}=\prod_{j\in F}X_{j},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

such that

.
So
f(U\,|\,a)=f_{F}(U_{F}\,|\,a)
.
In particular,
f
is determined by its marginals, as claimed.

that is compatible in the sense that

, for every clopen set

.
The limit property is therefore ensured.
The preservation under tensoring follows by arbitrariness of the family of Stone spaces.

Finally, we obtain our second main theorem, yielding a characterisation of infinite tensor products over
\mathsf{FinStoch}
.

Theorem 6.1

The ITP-preserving symmetric monoidal functor

\phi\colon{\mathsf{FinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}\to\mathsf{StoneStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}},

obtained by the inclusion
\mathsf{FinStoch}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{StoneStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
, is fully faithful.
Moreover, its essential image is given by infinite tensor products of finite sets in the sense of topological spaces.

In particular, by Brouwer’s theorem, any object in the essential image is isomorphic either to a finite set or to the Cantor space.
Moreover,
\phi
preserves the copy map because in both settings the finite marginalizations are deterministic. In other words, this functor is strong gs-monoidal in the sense of
[
12
]
.

3.1
, and faithfulness is immediate by

.

.
By
Proposition

5
,
f
is uniquely determined by
(f_{G})_{G\in\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{Y})}
, with
f_{G}\coloneqq\pi_{G}f
.
Let us now focus on
f_{G}
. Since it is locally constant,
f_{G}(U\,|\,-)
is constant on each member of a finite family
(A_{i}^{U})_{i=1,\dots,n_{U}}
. Since
Y_{G}
is finite, its subsets
U
are finite, so we obtain a finite family of clopens
(A_{i}^{U})_{i,\dots,n_{U},U\subseteq Y_{G}}
.

As any clopen in
X
is of the form
A_{F}\times\prod_{j\in J\setminus F}X_{j}
for some
F
, there is a finite set
F
common to all members of the finite family
(A_{i}^{U})_{i,U}
, such that each
A_{i}^{U}
can be written in such a cylindrical form.
In particular,
f_{G}(U\,|\,a)=f_{G}(U\,|\,b)
for any
b
such that
\pi_{F}(a)=\pi_{F}(b)
. In other words,
f_{G}
factors through
\pi_{F}
by definining, for any
\tilde{a}\in X_{F}
,
f_{F,G}(U\,|\,\tilde{a})\coloneqq f_{G}(U\,|\,a)
, where
a\in X
is any element such that
\pi_{F}(a)=\tilde{a}
.
It is now a direct check that the given definition of
f_{F,G}
results in a compatible family, therefore ensuring fullness.

Corollary 2

{\mathsf{BinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{CantorStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
, the full subcategory of
\mathsf{StoneStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
in which objects are
2^{N}=\{0,1\}^{N}
, where
N
is any natural number or
\mathbb{N}
.

In particular, by combining
Proposition

3
,
Corollary

2
, and
Example

5
, we obtain a characterisation by generators and equations of
\mathsf{CantorStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
.

Corollary 3

\mathsf{CantorStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Free}^{\infty}(\Sigma,E)
, where
(\Sigma,E)
is the symmetric monoidal theory of
{\mathsf{CausCirc}}
(
cf.

Example

5
).

Another important consequence of our results is that
\mathsf{CantorStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
includes all probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
.
This follows from the fact that
\mathbb{R}
is the infinite tensor power of
2
in
\mathsf{BorelStoch}

[
16
]
.
Indeed, since both
\mathbb{R}
in
\mathsf{BorelStoch}
and
2^{\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathsf{CantorStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
are associated to compatible families of morphisms
I\to 2^{n}
in
\mathsf{FinStoch}
, we obtain
\mathsf{BorelStoch}(I,\mathbb{R})\cong\mathsf{CantorStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}(I,2^{\mathbb{N}})
. This observation suggests that
\mathsf{CantorStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
is a rather expressive setting for studying continuous probability. We provide a simple use case below.

7
A Use Case of Plate Notation: Markov Chains

As an example of diagrammatic reasoning using plate notation, we briefly discuss Markov chains, which have been recently studied via Markov categories in
[
15
]
. We restrict here to the time-homogenous case, which defines a Markov chain with
n
steps in
\mathsf{BinStoch}
as an endomorphism
f\colon X\to X
inductively, as follows:

{\hbox to52.78pt{\vbox to10.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 26.38963pt\lower-5.18861pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.79 0 L 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{{{{{}}{

For example,
c_{3}
is given by the following string diagram:

f
X
X
f
X
f
X

The idea is that each copy of
X
represents a random variable of the chain, and the use of the same
f
for all steps translates to the request that
P(X_{n}\mid X_{n-1})=P(X_{n+1}\mid X_{n})
, i.e. that the obtained Markov chain is time-homogeneous.
Additional details can be found in
[
15
]
.

It can be verified that the
c_{n}
s defines a compatible family, hence a morphism
c\colon X\to X^{\mathbb{N}}
, the infinite tensor power of
X
. As one may expect,
c
is invariant under the addition of a precedent step in the inductive construction.
Note this property cannot be formulated in the discrete setting of
\mathsf{BinStoch}
(and of
{\mathsf{CausCirc}}
); however, we can easily prove it by diagrammatic reasoning in
{{\mathsf{CausCirc}}}^{\otimes\infty}
, as justified by
Corollary

3
:

where the last step is a simple reindexing of the compatible family defining
c
.

8
Conclusion and Future Work

This paper focuses on introducing categorical methods for studying continuous probabilistic processes as limits of discrete ones. We do so by introducing a universal construction that associates to any semicartesian category
\mathsf{C}
with cancellative deletions a semicartesian category
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
with all (countably) infinite tensor products. We discuss how axiomatic presentations for
\mathsf{C}
can be lifted to
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
, and introduce plate notation to manipulate as string diagrams
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
-morphisms.

As main case study, we focus on
\mathsf{FinStoch}
, whose infinite tensor construction
{\mathsf{FinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
we characterise in terms of Stone spaces and locally constant Markov kernels.
Furthermore, the existence of an axiomatic presentation of the subcategory
\mathsf{BinStoch}
allows us to derive one for
{\mathsf{BinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
. We can effectively use diagrammatic reasoning in
{\mathsf{BinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
to study any probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
. We give a very simple use case regarding Markov chains; clearly, this only scratches the surface of what can be studied in
{\mathsf{BinStoch}}^{\otimes\infty}
, which remains to be explored in follow-up work.

Moreover, our approach suggests ways towards axiomatising Markov kernels beyond local constancy. One such direction may be based on describing general Markov kernels in terms of locally constant ones, possibly restricting to standard Borel spaces. Another direction may be to investigate a universal construction that guarantees disintegration of measures, in the sense of having conditionals in Markov categories (
[
11
]
).

Additionally, studying locally constant Markov kernels could yield a description of probabilistic morphisms between Boolean algebras via Stone duality, which may constitute an insightful contribution in its own right.

Another interesting question is studying examples different from
\mathsf{FinStoch}
and
\mathsf{BinStoch}
. Natural candidates are Gaussian probability (
\mathsf{Gauss}
, as studied in
[
25
]
), Gaussian mixtures (
[
27
]
), or even non probabilistic examples of Markov categories, such as the category of finite sets and multivalued functions (
[
11
, Example 2.6]
).

Finally, we are interested in integrations of our approach (and of plate notation) with categorical approaches to probability that go beyond Markov categories, such as partial Markov categories
[
9
]
, tape diagrams
[
4
]
, and graded diagrams
[
23
]
.

Disclosure of Interests.

The authors have no competing interests to declare that are
relevant to the content of this article.

Appendix 0.B
Compatible Families Form a Semicartesian Category

This appendix shows that the universal construction
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
offered in
Example

4
indeed yields a semicartesian category.
We start with a precise definition of the composition, briefly sketched in
Definition

8
.

Definition 12

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a semicartesian category with cancellative deletion.
Let
X
,
Y
and
Z
be abstract infinite tensor products and let
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
be compatible families.

We define the
composition of compatible families

gf\colon X\to Y
by setting

\Lambda_{gf}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{X})\times\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{Z})
to be the set of pairs
(F,H)
for which there exists
G\in\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{Y})
such that
(F,G)\in\Lambda_{f}
and
(G,H)\in\Lambda_{g}
, and

gf_{F,H}\coloneqq g_{G,H}f_{F,G}

for any meaningful choice of
G
.

The proof that this is indeed a good notion of composition is easily shown.
Given two choices
G
and
G^{\prime}
, we can consider the following commutative diagram given by naturality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\tilde{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(G\cup G^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\tilde{F},G\cup G^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{G\cup G^{\prime},H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{F,G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{G,H}}

where
\tilde{F}
is obtained by the covering condition. Cancellativity of deletions then implies surjectivity of
X(\tilde{F})\to X(F)
, which in turn shows that the lower composition is completely determined by the upper composition, therefore concluding that
g_{G,H}f_{F,G}=g_{G^{\prime},H}f_{F,G^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 1

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a semicartesian category with cancellative deletion.
The equivalence of compatible families respects pointwise composition.

Proof

This follows from a reasoning similar to the one above: let
f,f^{\prime}\colon X\to Y
and
g,g^{\prime}\colon Y\to Z
be compatible families such that
f
is equivalent to
f^{\prime}
and
g
is equivalent to
g^{\prime}
.
Then, for each
H\in\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{fin}}(J_{Z})
, there exists
G
and
G^{\prime}
such that
g_{G,H}
and
g^{\prime}_{G^{\prime},H}
are defined.
By equivalence of
g
and
g^{\prime}
and hereditariness, we have
g_{G\cup G^{\prime},H}=g^{\prime}_{G\cup G^{\prime},H}
.
Now, a similar reasoning for
f
and
f^{\prime}
starting from
G\cup G^{\prime}
gives rise to a set
\tilde{F}
such that
f_{\tilde{F},G\cup G^{\prime}}=f^{\prime}_{\tilde{F},G\cup G^{\prime}}
.
Combining the two together, we proved that for every
H
there exists
\tilde{F}
such that
gf_{\tilde{F},H}=g^{\prime}f^{\prime}_{\tilde{F},H}
.
In particular, for each
(F,H)\in\Lambda_{gf}\cap\Lambda_{g^{\prime}f^{\prime}}
, we have
\tilde{F}\supseteq F
such that
gf_{\tilde{F},H}=g^{\prime}f^{\prime}_{\tilde{F},H}
, and by cancellativity of deletion we conclude that
gf_{{F},H}=g^{\prime}f^{\prime}_{{F},H}
as well.

Proposition 6

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a semicartesian category with cancellative deletions. Then
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
is a semicartesian category.

Proof

By
Lemma

1
,
{\mathsf{C}}^{\otimes\infty}
is a category.
The remaining properties follow by direct check: its symmetric monoidal structure is inherited from that of
\mathsf{C}
, with associators, unitors and permutations described pointwise, while semicartesianity follows from terminality of
I
in
\mathsf{C}
, by definition of compatible families.

be locally constant Markov kernels. Then their composition

gf(V\,|\,x)\coloneqq\int_{Y}g(V\,|\,y)f(dy\,|\,x)

is locally constant.

The proof is instructive as it sheds light on the “finiteness” of the notion.

Proof

Since
g(V\,|\,-)
is locally constant on a Stone space, we can construct a finite clopen cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
Y
where all clopens are disjoint and
g(V\,|\,-)_{|U_{i}}
is constant.
Then,

\int_{Y}g(V\,|\,y)f(dy\,|\,x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(V\,|\,U_{i})f(U_{i}\,|\,x)

we now define
A_{i}
the clopen set such that
x\in A_{i}
and
f(U_{i}\,|\,-)
is constant on
A_{i}
. Then, on
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
, the whole summation is constant, therefore concluding the proof.

To show that
\mathsf{StoneStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
is a Markov category, we need to ensure the existence of copy maps. In
\mathsf{Stoch}
, these are determined by the diagonal maps
X\to X\times X
. With this in mind, we show the following.

Any continuous function

.

Proof

That a continuous function yields a (continuous) Markov kernel is well-known, and follows by setting
\tilde{f}(U\,|\,x)\coloneqq\delta_{f(x)}(U)
for any continuous function
f\colon X\to Y
.
Then for any clopen
U
,
f^{-1}(U)
and its complement are sufficient to ensure that
\tilde{f}(U\,|\,-)
is locally constant.
The fact that
\tilde{f}(-\,|\,x)
is a finitely-additive probability measure is immediate.

Proposition 7

The category
\mathsf{StoneStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
, whose objects are Stone spaces and whose morphisms are locally constant Markov kernels, is a Markov subcategory of
\mathsf{Stoch}
.

In particular, since
\mathsf{Stoch}
has cancellative deletions, so does
\mathsf{StoneStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
.

Proof

By
Lemma

2
,
\mathsf{StoneStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
is indeed a category.
The tensor product of two locally constant Markov kernels is clearly a locally constant Markov kernel. Moreover, since all continuous maps give rise to locally constant Markov kernels (
Lemma

3
), copy and delete maps also belong to
\mathsf{StoneStoch}_{\mathsf{lc}}
, thus concluding the proof.
